II. STANDARD OF REVIEW

“A 12(b)(6) motion tests the sufficiency of the allegations contained in the complaint.”

Kost v. Kozakiewicz, 1 F.3d 176, 183 (3d Cir. 1993). In deciding a motion to dismiss under Rule 12(b)(6), the Court must determine whether the complaint contains “sufficient factual matter, state law requiring submission of an affidavit from a medical professional attesting to the suit’s merit has no application in federal court); see also Talley v. Pillai, 116 F.4th 200, 207 n.7 (3d Cir. 2024) (“A Pennsylvania malpractice claim is not ‘based’ on a COM.”).

8 Howard also provides an updated timeline of his events since filing his Complaint.

(ECF No. 41 at 5-7.) Although allegations filed outside the Complaint cannot be considered for the underlying plausibility of the claims, this information is included for background purposes only. Howard informs the Court that after experiencing severe bleeding, he received a blood test on September 6, 2024, which revealed his hemoglobin count had dropped. (Id. at 5.) Based on these results, Howard again requested to be seen by disease specialist Dr. Alejandro, which Dr.

Letizio denied. (Id.) Howard filed a grievance on September 20, 2024, based on this denial.

(Id.) Dr. Letizio followed up with Howard about his grievance and explained the Central Office contacted him about Howard’s current treatment. (Id. at 5-6.) After discussions with Howard about the treatment he has received, Dr. Letizio subsequently ordered a CAT scan for him and also told him that the disease specialist appointment would be approved. (Id. at 6.) Howard received the CAT scan on June 20, 2025, but did not receive an appointment with the disease specialist. (Id. at 7.) The CAT scan revealed two large lumps on his colon. (Id.) Based on these results, the new medical director ordered him to have an MRI since Dr. Letizio was no longer employed at SCI Phoenix. (Id.) Howard received an MRI, colonoscopy, and evaluation by a gastroenterologist on February 2, 2026. (Id.)

Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 5 of 19

6 accepted as true, to ‘state a claim to relief that is plausible on its face.’” Ashcroft v. Iqbal, 556 U.S. 662, 678 (2009) (quoting Bell Atl. Corp. v. Twombly, 550 U.S. 544, 570 (2007)). “A claim has facial plausibility when the plaintiff pleads factual content that allows the court to draw the reasonable inference that the defendant is liable for the misconduct alleged.” Id. (citing Twombly, 550 U.S. at 556). “Threadbare recitals of the elements of a cause of action, supported by mere conclusory statements, do not suffice.” Id. (citing Twombly, 550 U.S. at 555.)

“Although the plausibility standard does not impose a probability requirement, it does require a pleading to show more than a sheer possibility that a defendant has acted unlawfully.” Connelly

v. Lane Const. Corp., 809 F.3d 780, 786 (3d Cir. 2016) (internal quotation marks and citations omitted). It is the defendant’s burden to show that a complaint fails to state a claim. See Hedges

v. United States, 404 F.3d 744, 750 (3d Cir. 2005) (explaining that on a Rule 12(b)(6) motion to dismiss, the “defendant bears the burden of showing that no claim has been presented”).

In resolving a Rule 12(b)(6) motion, “a court must consider only the complaint, exhibits attached to the complaint, matters of public record, as well as undisputedly authentic documents if the complainant’s claims are based upon these documents.” Mayer v. Belichick, 605 F.3d 223,

230 (3d Cir. 2010). To determine whether a complaint filed by a pro se litigant states a claim, the Court must accept the facts alleged in the pro se Complaint as true, draw all reasonable inferences in the plaintiff’s favor, and ask only whether the Complaint contains facts sufficient to state a plausible claim. See Shorter v. United States, 12 F.4th 366, 374 (3d Cir. 2021), abrogation on other grounds recognized by Fisher v. Hollingsworth, 115 F.4th 197 (3d Cir.

2024); see also Vogt v. Wetzel, 8 F.4th 182, 185 (3d Cir. 2021) (pro se filings are construed liberally).

Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 6 of 19

7

Dismissal of a complaint based on an affirmative defense, such as res judicata, is only appropriate at the pleading stage when the “defense is apparent on the face of the complaint.”

Wisniewski v. Fisher, 857 F.3d 152, 157 (3d Cir. 2017). “[W]hile a court may entertain a motion to dismiss on [affirmative defenses], it may not allocate . . . [burdens] . . . in a way that is inconsistent with the rule that a plaintiff is not required to plead, in a complaint, facts sufficient to overcome an affirmative defense.” Schmidt v. Skolas, 770 F.3d 241, 251 (3d Cir. 2014).

Rather, the burden of pleading and proving an affirmative defense is borne by the defendant.

Williams v. Runyon, 130 F.3d 568, 573 (3d Cir. 1997).

III. DISCUSSION

Howard asserts violations of his constitutional rights, as well as violations of state law.

“To state a claim under § 1983, a plaintiff must allege the violation of a right secured by the Constitution and laws of the United States, and must show that the alleged deprivation was committed by a person acting under color of state law.” West v. Atkins, 487 U.S. 42, 48 (1988).

“A defendant in a civil rights action must have personal involvement in the alleged wrongs” to be liable. See Rode v. Dellarciprete, 845 F.2d 1195, 1207 (3d Cir. 1988).

A. Preclusion

Defendants assert that Howard is precluded from rearguing the issue of whether he received adequate care for his intestinal issues because the Court already dismissed a prior case regarding that care. See Howard v. Wellpath, No. 22-3241, 2023 WL2226797, at *1 (E.D. Pa.

Feb. 24, 2023). Howard responds in opposition arguing claim preclusion does not prevent him from moving forward with these claims. In an abundance of caution, the Court will address both issue preclusion and claim preclusion.

Federal courts look to state law in determining whether to apply collateral estoppel, Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 7 of 19

8 also referred to as issue preclusion. See Marrese v. Am. Acad. of Orthopaedic Surgeons, 470 U.S. 373, 381 (1985). Collateral estoppel bars the re-litigation of a previously litigated issue.

See Vignola v. Vignola, 39 A.3d 390, 393 (Pa. Super. Ct, 2012). Collateral estoppel “is a broader concept than res judicata and operates to prevent a question of law or issue of fact which has once been litigated and fully determined in a court of competent jurisdiction from being relitigated in a subsequent suit.” Id. (citation omitted.) Under Pennsylvania law, the following conditions must be met in order to invoke issue preclusion: (1) the issue decided in the prior adjudication was identical with the one presented in the later action; (2) there was a final judgment on the merits; (3) the party against whom issue preclusion is asserted was a party or in privity with a party to the prior adjudication; and (4) the party against whom it is asserted has had a full and fair opportunity to litigate the issue in question in a prior action; and (5) whether the determination in the prior proceeding was essential to the judgment. Id.

Turning to claim preclusion, also called res judicata, which “protects defendants from the risk of repetitious suits involving the same cause of action once a court of competent jurisdiction has entered a final judgment on the merits.” Beasley v. Howard, 14 F.4th 226, 231 (3d Cir.

2021) (internal quotations omitted). Claim preclusion prevents parties from raising issues that could have been raised and decided in a prior case regardless of whether those issues were litigated. Id. In other words, “[t]he prior judgment’s preclusive effect . . . extends not only to the claims that the plaintiff brought in the first action, but also to any claims the plaintiff could have asserted in the previous lawsuit.” Id. at 231-32. “Claim preclusion similarly reaches theories of recovery: a plaintiff who asserts a different theory of recovery in a separate lawsuit cannot avoid claim preclusion when the events underlying the two suits are essentially the same.” Id. at 232; see also Sheridan v. NGK Metals Corp., 609 F.3d 239, 261 (3d Cir. 2010) (“Rather than resting Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 8 of 19

9 on the specific legal theory invoked, res judicata generally is thought to turn on the essential similarity of the underlying events giving rise to the various legal claims.” (quoting United States

v. Athlone Indus., Inc., 746 F.2d 977, 983-84 (3d Cir. 1984))). Three elements are required for claim preclusion to apply: “(1) a final judgment on the merits in a prior suit involving (2) the same parties or their privies and (3) a subsequent suit based on the same cause of action.” In re Healthcare Real Est. Partners, LLC, 941 F. 3d 64, 72 (3d Cir. 2019) (quoting In re Mullarkey,

536 F.3d 215, 225 (3d Cir. 2008)).

In the prior action, the Court dismissed Howard’s deliberate indifference claims against Wellpath, Dr. Letizio, Huner, and another medical provider, Dr. Bora, for failure to state a claim.

See Howard, 2023 WL2226797, at *1. Howard’s allegations in that case, relevant to Defendants here, were largely based on Dr. Letizio’s failure to follow up with a gastroenterologist at Temple University, who diagnosed Howard with a small intestinal infection and prescribed antibiotics in 2022. Id. at *2. Howard claimed the antibiotics made his infection and symptoms worse, and after several sick calls and a grievance filed about his issues, Dr. Letizio told him in April 2022 that they would contact the gastroenterologist about his pain. Id. However, Dr. Letizio never followed up with Howard about whether he reached out, or received a response from the gastroenterologist, and, thus, failed to treat him. Id. He also claimed he dealt with this intestinal pain for thirteen years and the medical staff at SCI Phoenix are familiar with his symptoms dating back to treatment received since 2010. Id. at *2-3. The Complaint in this case does not reallege any factual allegations occurring in 2022, or any that are based on his appointment with the Temple University gastroenterologist, and/or Dr. Letizio’s subsequent failure to treat him after he received antibiotics. Importantly, it was not determined that Howard did not actually have an intestinal disorder as Defendants insinuate. Rather, the Court just concluded that the Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 9 of 19

10

Defendants were not deliberately indifferent to his needs relating to the care received at that time. This is distinguishable from the case cited by Defendants, Kokinda v. Pa. Dept. of Corrections, 803 Fed.Appx. 574, 577 (3d Cir. 2020), which precluded Kokinda from bringing a new deliberate indifference case related to not receiving a certain diet when a prior case concluded that such diet was not medically necessary for his serious medical needs.

In contrast, this action is premised on Dr. Letizio’s denial for Howard to see a disease specialist after Dr. Stanek, a gastroenterologist at Einstein Gastroenterology, recommended him to do so in November 2023. (Compl. ¶¶ 8, 9.) Although the same parties are sued here, with the exception of Dr. Bora, and the issues relate to Howard’s complaints about his intestinal pain, this cause of action is based on events related to a denial to see disease specialist nearly two years after he saw the Temple University gastroenterologist. Furthermore, the fact that Howard saw Dr. Stanek, who recommended he see an infectious disease specialist based on his complications, demonstrates that the circumstances related to his medical needs for his intestinal pain have changed since 2022. Thus, the issues raised here cannot be determined from the face of the Complaint as “identical” or based on the same cause of action regarding his treatment challenged in the 2022 case. Accordingly, preclusion is inapplicable and Defendants’ motions will be denied as to this ground.

B. Official Capacity Claims

1. Defendant Huner

Huner moves to dismiss Howard’s claims for damages against her in her official capacity as barred by the Eleventh Amendment. (ECF No. 27 at 7-9.) She also argues that the official capacity claims for damages are barred because state employees in their official capacities are Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 10 of 19

11 not “persons” subject to liability under § 1983. (Id. at 9.) Howard does not respond to this argument in his opposition brief.

Official capacity claims are indistinguishable from claims against the governmental entity that employs Huner, which is the Commonwealth here. See Kentucky v. Graham, 473 U.S. 159, 165-66 (1985) (“Official-capacity suits . . . ‘generally represent only another way of pleading an action against an entity of which an officer is an agent.’” (quoting Monell v. N.Y.C. Dept. of Soc.

Servs., 436 U.S. 658, 690, n. 55 (1978))). Thus, § 1983 claims against Huner in her official capacity are really claims against the Commonwealth, which is shielded from such claims by Eleventh Amendment immunity and, in any event, is not considered a “person” subject to liability under § 1983. See Downey v. Pa. Dep’t. of Corr., 968 F.3d 299, 309-10 (3d Cir. 2020) (“Eleventh Amendment immunity bars actions for retroactive relief against state officers acting in their official capacity.”); see also Lavia v. Pa. Dep’t of Corr., 224 F.3d 190, 195 (3d Cir.

2000) (explaining that, “[b]ecause the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania’s Department of Corrections is a part of the executive department of the Commonwealth, it shares in the Commonwealth’s Eleventh Amendment immunity” and is also not considered a person for purposes of § 1983). Accordingly, the Court will grant Huner’s motion to dismiss Howard’s official capacity claims against her.

2. Defendant Dr. Letizio

The “official capacity” claims against Dr. Letizio, a Wellpath employee, are also not cognizable because Wellpath is a private entity. See Kreis v. Northampton Cnty. Prison, No. 21- 2360, 2022 WL 4236692, at *8 (E.D. Pa. Sept. 14, 2022) (stating that official capacity claims are “inapplicable to suits against private parties where the entity is also susceptible to suit” (citation omitted.)) Even if official capacity suits against individuals who work for private companies Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 11 of 19

12 were cognizable, the suit would, in effect, be one against the company for whom that individual works, which is Wellpath here. See Graham, 473 U.S. at 105. Since Howard concedes the claims against Wellpath should be dismissed (see ECF No. 41 at 1), the official capacity claims against Dr. Letizio are duplicative of those claims and are also dismissed. Accord Burk v. West, No. 21-4968, 2021 WL 5758945, at *2 (E.D. Pa. Nov. 24, 2021).

C. Failure to State an Eighth Amendment Claim

Defendants Huner and Dr. Letizio next argue that Howard has failed to state a plausible Eighth Amendment claim against them. To state a constitutional claim under the Eighth Amendment based on the failure to provide medical treatment, a prisoner must allege facts indicating that prison officials were deliberately indifferent to his serious medical needs. See Farmer v. Brennan, 511 U.S. 825, 835 (1994). A prison official is not deliberately indifferent “unless the official knows of and disregards an excessive risk to inmate health or safety; the official must both be aware of facts from which the inference could be drawn that a substantial risk of serious harm exists, and he must also draw the inference.” Id. at 837. “A medical need is serious, . . . if it is one that has been diagnosed by a physician as requiring treatment or one that is so obvious that a lay person would easily recognize the necessity for a doctor’s attention.”

Monmouth Cnty. Corr. Institutional Inmates v. Lanzaro, 834 F.2d 326, 347 (3d Cir. 1987) (internal quotations omitted). Deliberate indifference is properly alleged “where the prison official (1) knows of a prisoner’s need for medical treatment but intentionally refuses to provide it; (2) delays necessary medical treatment based on a non-medical reason; or (3) prevents a prisoner from receiving needed or recommended medical treatment.” Rouse v. Plantier, 182 F.3d 192, 197 (3d Cir. 1999). A serious medical need exists where “failure to treat can be expected to lead to substantial and unnecessary suffering.” Colburn v. Upper Darby Township, Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 12 of 19

13

946 F.2d 1017, 1023 (3d Cir. 1991). This standard is met when a delay or denial of medical care is intended to inflict pain without medical justification or is based solely on a nonmedical reason without any effort to mitigate harm. DiFraia v. Ransom, 171 F.4th 622, 630 (3d Cir. 2026). In contrast, allegations of medical malpractice and mere disagreement regarding proper medical treatment are insufficient to establish a constitutional violation. See Spruill v. Gillis, 372 F.3d 218, 235 (3d Cir. 2004); see also Durmer v. O’ Carroll, 991 F.2d 64, 67 (3d Cir. 1993).

Inadequate care may nevertheless give rise to a deliberate indifference claim when the Defendants “act with the requisite state of mind when providing that inadequate care.” Pearson

v. Prison Health Serv., 850 F.3d 526, 535 (3d Cir. 2017).

1. Defendant Huner

Huner argues that the Complaint fails to allege she had any personal involvement in his medical treatment and Howard premises his claims against her based on her supervisory position at SCI Phoenix. (See ECF No. 27 at 9-12.) She further argues Howard has failed to state a claim that she was deliberately indifferent to his serious medical needs. (Id. at 12-14.) Howard’s response does not refute Huner’s arguments or offer any additional clarification as to Huner and her involvement relevant to his claims, referring to “Defendants” collectively, such as the “Defendants denied a gastroenterologist orders for [him] to be examined by a disease specialist” and Defendants have “yet to diagnose the source of what [is] causing” his gastrointestinal issues.” (ECF No. 41 at 4.) He points to Dr. Letizio and his actions throughout his opposition but does not make any argument in response to Huner’s motion, or even state her name once throughout his response.

To state a § 1983 claim, as noted, Howard must allege how each defendant was personally involved in the events and occurrences giving rise to the claims brought against that Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 13 of 19

14 defendant. See Rode, 845 F.2d at 1207; see also Jutrowski v. Township of Riverdale, 904 F.3d 280, 290 (3d Cir. 2018) (“Each Government official, his or her title notwithstanding, is only liable for his or her own misconduct.” (quoting Iqbal, 556 U.S. at 677)). “A plaintiff makes sufficient allegations of a defendant’s personal involvement by describing the defendant’s participation in or actual knowledge of and acquiescence in the wrongful conduct.” Chavarriaga

v. N.J. Dep’t of Corr., 806 F.3d 210, 222 (3d Cir. 2015) (citing Rode, 845 F.3d at 1207).

“Although a court can infer that a defendant had contemporaneous knowledge of wrongful conduct from the circumstances surrounding a case, the knowledge must be actual, not constructive.” Id. (citation omitted). Furthermore, generalized allegations that a supervisory defendant is “in charge of” or “responsible for” an office or facility are insufficient to allege personal involvement in an underlying constitutional violation. See Saisi v. Murray, 822 F.

App’x 47, 48 (3d Cir. 2020) (per curiam) (“Saisi asserted that some defendants were ‘in charge of agencies that allowed this to happen,’ and that liability stemmed merely from defendants’ ‘belief’ that their conduct would be ‘tolerated.’ However, a director cannot be held liable ‘simply because of his position as the head of the [agency].’” (quoting Evancho v. Fisher, 423 F.3d 347, 354 (3d Cir. 2005)). A plaintiff cannot predicate liability for a § 1983 claim on a respondeat superior basis. Chavarriaga, 806 F.3d at 227 (citing Rode, 845 F.2d at 1207).

Instead, in § 1983 actions, “[s]uits against high-level government officials must satisfy the general requirements for supervisory liability.” Wharton v. Danberg, 854 F.3d 234, 243 (3d Cir.

2017).

There are “two general ways in which a supervisor-defendant may be liable for unconstitutional acts undertaken by subordinates.” Barkes v. First Corr. Med., Inc., 766 F.3d 307, 316 (3d Cir. 2014), reversed on other grounds by Taylor v. Barkes, 575 U.S. 822 (2015).

Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 14 of 19

15

First, a supervisor may be liable if he or she “with deliberate indifference to the consequences, established and maintained a policy, practice or custom which directly caused [the] constitutional harm.” Id. (quoting A.M. ex rel. J.M.K. v. Luzerne Cnty. Juvenile Det. Ctr., 372 F.3d 572, 586 (3d Cir. 2004) (alteration in original)). The second way a supervisor may be personally liable for unconstitutional acts taken by subordinates is if “he or she participated in violating the plaintiff’s rights, directed others to violate them, or, as the person in charge, had knowledge of and acquiesced in the subordinate’s unconstitutional conduct.” Id.

Howard’s Complaint alleges that in response to his letter sent to the DOC Central Office complaining about Dr. Letizio denying him to see a disease specialist, Benning responded that she was forwarding his initial letter and her response to Huner “for her to review and resolve Dr.

Letizio’s denial.” (Compl. ¶ 12.) Howard claims Huner has not contacted him about “the rectifying of Letizio’s denial (or to acknowledge that she received [his] letter to [the] Central Office and Ms. Benning’s response to it) and/or to respond to [his] requests to staff concerning the denial.” (Id. ¶ 13.) He also asserts that as the Chief Health Care Administrator, she oversees “the medical staff, personnel and handling problems inmates have with the medical department and/or staff in general.” (Id. at 2, ¶ 7.) As an initial matter, it is not clear if Huner received the letter from Benning or even knew about Dr. Letizio’s denial for him to see the disease specialist.

His own allegations aver that Huner did not acknowledge receiving this letter and he relied on Benning, a third party, to notify her about his issues. He does not allege that he made any additional efforts during the relevant period of time to contact Huner or describe any other specific involvement she may have had in providing his care. As pled, this does not sufficiently allege Huner was personally involved in, or even knew, about Dr. Letizio’s appointment denial.

He also appears to attempt to impose liability upon Huner based on general allegations that she is Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 15 of 19

16 in charge of health care operations at SCI Phoenix, which he cannot do. See Saisi, 822 F. App’x at 48. Because he has presented insufficient allegations regarding her involvement in his medical treatment, it follows that he has not pled a plausible supervisory liability claim. See, e.g., Iqbal, 556 U.S. at 678; see also Martinez v. UPMC Susquehanna, 986 F.3d 261, 266 (3d Cir. 2021) (“A plaintiff cannot survive dismissal just by alleging the conclusion to an ultimate legal issue.”). Absent allegations supporting an inference that Huner was aware of Howard’s issues pertaining to seeing a specialist or that she played a role in denying that care, Howard also cannot allege that she was deliberately indifferent.9

Thus, the deliberate indifference claim against Huner is not plausible and her motion to dismiss this claim will be granted.10

9 The Court notes even had Howard alleged that Huner received the letter from Benning and knew about Dr. Letizio’s denial, she still could not be held liable for deliberate indifference as non-medical provider. Claims based on inadequate medical care against a non-medical provider, like Huner, fail because Howard alleges that he is under the care of medical staff at SCI Phoenix. Courts have held that “[i]f a prisoner is under the care of medical experts . . . , a nonmedical prison official will generally be justified in believing that the prisoner is in capable hands.” Spruill, 372 F.3d at 236 (“Inmate health and safety is promoted by dividing responsibility for various aspects of inmate life among guards, administrators, physicians, and so on. Holding a non-medical prison official liable in a case where a prisoner was under a physician’s care would strain this division of labor.”); see also, e.g., Stewart v. Pa. Dep’t of Corr., 677 F. App’x 816, 819 (3d Cir. 2017) (per curiam) (affirming dismissal of claims where plaintiff “claimed that these Defendants, supervisory officials who do not participate in individual medical care decisions, simply failed to intervene in his medical care” because “[s]uch a claim is not viable under the Eighth Amendment”); Carter v. Smith, 483 F. App’x 705, 708 (3d Cir. 2012) (per curiam) (“Prison officials cannot be held to be deliberately indifferent merely because they did not respond to the medical complaints of a prisoner who was already being treated by the prison medical staff.”).

10 Huner also argues in support of dismissal for a negligence claim, but Howard only alleges the negligence claim against Wellpath. (Compl. at 7.) In any event, a state law negligence claim against her would be barred by sovereign immunity for the reasons stated in her motion, and a professional negligence claim would also fail because she is not licensed medical provider. See Pa. R. Civ. P. 1042.3(a) (“In any action based upon an allegation that a licensed professional deviated from an acceptable professional standard…”) (emphasis added); see also, see also, e.g., Poteat v. Lydon, No. 22-2114, 2023 WL 6620368, at *3 (3d Cir. Oct. 11, 2023) (per curiam) (“State sovereign immunity bars these suits against the Commonwealth and its employees acting within the scope of their duties, as the PSP employees were, and the limited Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 16 of 19

17

2. Defendant Dr. Letizio

As explained, Howard alleges on November 29, 2023, he was taken to an appointment with Dr. Stanek, a gastroenterologist, at Einstein Gastroenterology. (Compl. ¶ 8.) After reviewing his symptoms and medical history, Dr. Stanek told him that he may have an undiagnosed intestinal disease that has been overlooked and “would have to be diagnosed and/or treated by a disease specialist.” (Id.) Dr. Stanek ordered Howard to be seen by Dr. Alejandro, a disease specialist at the Einstein Infection Disease Center. (Id.) Howard alleges Dr. Letizio subsequently “denied the gastroenterologist’s orders.” (Id. ¶ 9.) These allegations indicate that Dr. Letizio was aware of Howard’s need for further treatment, which was to see a disease specialist due to a serious medical need, and he denied Howard such treatment. Although it is unclear why Dr. Letizio denied Dr. Stanek’s order for Howard to see a disease specialist, the deliberate indifference claim based on the allegations as pled may proceed past this stage of the proceedings because they support an inference that Dr. Letizio denied medically indicated care for Howard. While a doctor’s exercise of medical judgment in providing care precludes a finding of deliberate indifference, these allegations, read in the light most favorable to Howard suggest that Dr. Letizio was not exercising medical judgment when he denied Howard from seeing a disease specialist after Dr. Stanek, a gastroenterologist, opined he needed such care after negligence exceptions to sovereign immunity do not apply as Poteat alleged only intentional torts.”); Kidd v. Pennsylvania, 37 F. App’x 588, 592 n.3 (3d Cir. 2002) (summarily dismissing an intentional infliction of emotional distress claim because “[t]he Commonwealth, it[s] subdivisions, and its employees acting within the scope of employment enjoy Eleventh Amendment immunity in federal court. The Pennsylvania legislature has not chosen to waive this immunity for intentional torts.” (citation omitted)); Rightmyer v. Philly Pregnancy Ctr., P.C., No. 23-1925, 2024 WL 896571, at *3 (E.D. Pa. Mar. 1, 2024) (“As a licensed registered nurse and not a licensed physician, Ms. Swann does not meet the substantive requirements of § 1303.512 and, thus, her [Certificate of Merit] does not meet the requirements of Pennsylvania Rule 1042.3.”).

Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 17 of 19

18 examining Howard.11 See Merritt v. Fogel, 349 F. App’x 742, 746 (3d Cir. 2009) (per curiam) (vacating dismissal of complaint despite the Magistrate Judge’s observation that “defendants have repeatedly monitored and tested [plaintiff] and have determined that he does not qualify for HCV treatment” when other allegations reflected defendants falsely represented that plaintiff was never within the protocol for treatment despite prior recommendation from specialists and made comments arguably suggestive of deliberate indifference); see also Inmates of Allegheny Cnty. Jail v. Pierce, 612 F.2d 754, 762 (3d Cir. 1979) (explaining that, while courts will “disavow any attempt to second-guess the propriety or adequacy of a particular course of treatment[,] which remains a question of sound professional judgment[, i]mplicit in this deference to prison medical authorities is the assumption that such an informed judgment has, in fact, been made” (citation modified)). In sum, Dr. Letizio has not met his burden for obtaining dismissal on the merits, and the deliberate indifference claim against him is sufficient to proceed to discovery.

IV. CONCLUSION

For the foregoing reasons, Huner’s Motion to Dismiss will be granted and the claims against her will be dismissed with prejudice. The Motion to Dismiss filed by Wellpath and Dr.

Letizio will be granted in part and denied in part. The Motion will be granted as to the claims against Wellpath and denied as to the Eighth Amendment medical indifference claim against Dr.

Letizio. Huner and Wellpath will be terminated as a defendant in this case. All official capacity claims and Fourteenth Amendment claims will also be dismissed. The Court will direct Dr.

11 It is also worth noting that considering the long-standing history and knowledge of Howard’s condition based on seeing medical staff for this condition over the years, Dr. Letizio was well aware of Howard’s complications related to the underlying medical issues.

Case 2:24-cv-04839-JLS Document 42 Filed 09/18/26 Page 18 of 19

19

Letizio to file a responsive pleading as to remaining claim, which is the Eighth Amendment medical indifference claim against him.

An appropriate Order follows.

**Order to Show Cause, Langworthy v. Alcon**

10,003 tokens · 39,785 characters

IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT

FOR THE DISTRICT OF NEW MEXICO

GENEVA LANGWORTHY, )

)

Plaintiff, )

)

v. ) No. 1:24-cv-1291-MLG-KRS

)

EDWARD ALCON, )

)

Defendant. )

ORDER TO SHOW CAUSE

THIS MATTER is before the Court sua sponte upon review of the docket. For the reasons set forth below, the Court ORDERS Plaintiff to SHOW CAUSE why her claims against Defendant Alcon in the currently operative state court complaint (“Complaint”) (Doc. 1-2) should not be dismissed by filing an amended complaint that alleges those claims in compliance with the Federal and Local Rules of Civil Procedure.

BACKGROUND

The Court incorporates herein the discussion of the facts and procedural history of this case set forth in the Court’s Order Striking Pro Se Motions And Permission To File Electronically (Doc.

62). For present purposes, the Court sets forth the three claims alleged in the Complaint against Defendant Alcon, two based on the Fourth Amendment and one based on the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment.1

1 Plaintiff’s equal protection claim arises directly under the Fourteenth Amendment, while her Fourth Amendment claim technically also arises under the Fourteenth Amendment although indirectly through the Supreme Court’s incorporation of the Bill of Rights into that Amendment. See Colbruno v. Kessler, 928 F.3d 1155, 1161 (10th Cir.

2019) (explaining that “almost any federal constitutional claim against state officials is, strictly speaking, a claim under the Fourteenth Amendment,” but that “it is common to say,” for the sake of clarity, “that a claim is based, say, on the First Amendment, Fourth Amendment, Sixth Amendment, or Eighth Amendment” (internal citations omitted)).

Also, although it is not necessary for Plaintiff to allege a legal theory in the Complaint, the vehicle for Plaintiff to bring her constitutional claims is 42 U.S.C. § 1983. See Pahls v. Thomas, 718 F.3d 1210, 1225 (10th Cir. 2013) (stating that 42 U.S.C. § 1983 provides a cause of action against state officials who violate constitutional or other federally Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 1 of 17

2

The first Fourth Amendment claim is alleged in the following two paragraphs:

1. In the fall of 2021, Deputy Edward Alcon trespassed repeatedly on Geneva Langworthy’s property at 12 1/2 Towhee Lane without probable cause, in violation of the Plaintiff’s 4th amendment right against unlawful search and seizure. Deputy Alcon searched for Ms. Langworthy ON HER OWN PROPERTY with a powerful flashlight. There was no warrant for her arrest, and she was on her lawful property. The outrageous actions of Alcon have yet to be explained.

On the same date, Alcon and Tatum both drove their vehicles, with Tatum leading the way, over to Ms. Langworthy’s house on Jaybird Lane. Alcon and Tatum parked, illegally entered Ms. Langworthy’s property, and walked around inside her yard, shining their flashlights into the house and around the grounds in an outrageous and illegal warrantless search of her property, with no hint of probabl[e] cause.

(Doc. 1-2 at 2-3).

The second Fourth Amendment claim is set forth in the following three paragraphs:

2. In the fall of 2022, Deputy Alcon trespassed on

Jaybird Lane, a private road, in the company of local buffoon Todd Tatum, a fraud and a liar who has been unlawfully posing as “security” for Ute Park since 2015. Tatum is not licensed to be a security guard in New Mexico, nor does he have any right to claim to be that. Tatum routinely trespasses on all the roads in Ute Park with no authority whatsoever, and this fraud will be the subject of a new cause of action in the near future.

Alcon trespassed on Jaybird Lane in the fall of 2022 with fraudster Tatum in his patrol vehicle, and spotlighted

Ms. Langworthy in her yard as he drove past. This caused emotional distress to Ms. Langworthy as well as violating her property rights and her right to quiet enjoyment. It’s also harassment.

Ms. Langworthy has a video recording of the event, which she will be petitioning the court to receive into evidence.

protected rights); Pueblo of Pojoaque v. New Mexico, 214 F. Supp. 3d 1028, 1075 (D.N.M. 2016) (“Section 1983 authorizes an injured person to assert a claim for relief against a person who, acting under color of state law, violated the claimant’s federally protected rights.”), aff’d, 863 F.3d 1226 (10th Cir. 2017).

Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 2 of 17

3

Langworthy trespassed Todd Tatum from Jaybird Lane shortly after that, sending a copy of the letter to the Colfax County Sheriff.

(Id. at 3).

Plaintiff’s third and final claim under the Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment is set forth in the following two paragraphs:

3. In the winter of 2023, Ms. Langworthy attempted to press charges against Tatum for continuing to trespass on her road and to harass and intimidate her. The call was assigned to Alcon, who completely denied assistance or protection to Ms. Langworthy.

Alcon said he didn’t know whether the road was private. This is gross incompetence.

Denial of equal protection occurs when one person is treated differently than another, under color of law. When Tatum called in Deputy Alcon, the deputy hunted Ms. Langworthy down on her own property, in a warrantless search in violation of her 4th Amendment rights. When Langworthy called on Alcon to enforce the trespassing of Todd Tatum from Jaybird Lane, Alcon did not even take a complaint. Ms. Langworthy demands relief and protection.

(Id. at 3-4).

DISCUSSION

The Court has identified the following deficiencies, and orders Plaintiff to show cause why the Court should not dismiss the Complaint for failing to allege facts showing that Plaintiff is entitled to relief under either the Fourth Amendment or Fourteenth Amendment. See FED.R. CIV.

P. 8(a)(2); Lowrey v. Sandoval Cnty. Children Youth & Families Dep’t, No. 23-2035, 2023 WL 4560223, at *2 (10th Cir. July 17, 2023) (unpublished) (“Given a referral for non-dispositive Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 3 of 17

4 pretrial matters, a magistrate judge may point out deficiencies in the complaint … [and] order a litigant to show cause[.]” (citing 28 U.S.C. § 636(b)(1)(A); FED. R. CIV. P. 72(a))).2

A. FOURTH AMENDMENT CLAIMS

The Fourth Amendment provides in relevant part that “[t]he right of the people to be secure in their persons, houses, papers, and effects, against unreasonable searches and seizures, shall not be violated.” U.S. CONST. AMEND. IV. The cursory allegations in the Complaint, quoted in full above, may fit the description of “short and plain,” but they do not provide sufficient details to satisfy the further requirement in Rule 8 that the short and plain statement “show[ ] that [Plaintiff] is entitled to relief.” FED. R. CIV. P. 8(a)(2). The Complaint’s allegations state only that Alcon trespassed on Plaintiff’s property, without any facts to indicate that, in doing so, Alcon subjected Plaintiff to a search or seizure of her person, house, papers, or effects. Indeed, the above-quoted allegations may not even be sufficient to show that a trespass occurred. The Court of Appeals of New Mexico has defined trespass as a physical invasion of property that directly infringes on another’s right of possession. Dolan v. Fed. Emergency Mgmt. Agency, 760 F. Supp. 3d 1200, 1247 (D.N.M. 2024) (citing Padilla v. Lawrence, 1984-NMCA-064, ¶ 26, 101 N.M. 556, 563, 685 P.2d 964, 971); see also RESTATEMENT (SECOND) OF TORTS § 158 (1965) (“One is subject to liability to another for trespass, irrespective of whether he thereby causes harm to any legally protected interest of the other, if he intentionally … enters land in the possession of the other”).

The 2021 trespass allegedly occurred on “Geneva Langworthy’s property” located at 12 1/2 Towhee Lane. But the 2022 trespass allegedly occurred “on Jaybird Lane,” and, unlike 12 1/2 Towhee Lane, the Complaint does not allege that Jaybird Lane is “Geneva Langworthy’s

2 The Court is aware that Defendant Alcon has filed a motion for summary judgment based on qualified immunity.

See (Doc. 46). Before the Court can address the issue of qualified immunity, however, there must be statement of a claim which shows that Plaintiff “is entitled to relief.” FED.R.CIV. P. 8(a)(2).

Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 4 of 17

5 property.” Instead, it alleges only that Jaybird Lane is a “private road,” a statement too vague for the Court to infer that Plaintiff holds an exclusive possessory interest in it. And if Plaintiff did not possess a property right to preclude Alcon from driving his vehicle onto Jaybird Land, then both the trespass claim and any Fourth Amendment claim based on the 2022 incident would be legally insufficient, notwithstanding the additional allegation that Alcon “spotlighted Ms. Langworthy in her yard as he drove past.” See, e.g., United States v. Hay, 95 F.4th 1304, 1314 (10th Cir. 2024) (“viewing settings that are in public view, or visible via generally available technology, does not constitute a search”).3

Assuming that, as to the 2021 incident at least, Plaintiff has alleged facts to support a trespass claim, a trespass does not itself amount to a violation of the Fourth Amendment. “[T]he Supreme Court has made it clear that the Fourth Amendment does not track property law,”4 and, indeed, has held in multiple cases “that a trespass by law-enforcement officers did not violate the Fourth Amendment.” Rieck v. Jensen, 651 F.3d 1188, 1191 (10th Cir. 2011).

5 For instance,

3 See generally California v. Ciraolo, 476 U.S. 207, 213 (1986) (“The Fourth Amendment protection of the home has never been extended to require law enforcement officers to shield their eyes when passing by a home on public thoroughfares. Nor does the mere fact that an individual has taken measures to restrict some views of his activities preclude an officer’s observations from a public vantage point where he has a right to be and which renders the activities clearly visible.”).

4 See, e.g., United States v. Karo, 468 U.S. 705, 712-13 (1984) (“The existence of a physical trespass is only marginally relevant to the question of whether the Fourth Amendment has been violated, however, for an actual trespass is neither necessary nor sufficient to establish a constitutional violation.”); Oliver v. United States, 466 U.S. 170, 183-84, (1984) (“[T]he general rights of property protected by the common law of trespass have little or no relevance to the applicability of the Fourth Amendment.”); Katz v. United States, 389 U.S. 347, 353 (1967) (“[T]he premise that property interests control the right of the Government to search and seize has been discredited.” (quoting Warden v.

Hayden, 387 U.S. 294, 304 (1967)); see also United States v. Alabi, 943 F. Supp. 2d 1201, 1235 (D.N.M. 2013) (“the Fourth Amendment does not cover every investigation [ ] on private property” (internal quotation marks and citation omitted), aff’d, 597 F. App’x 991 (10th Cir. 2015).

5 See, e.g., Oliver, 66 U.S. 170 (holding that officers did not violate the Fourth Amendment by their warrantless intrusion on secluded property despite the officers having walked around a locked gate and various “No Trespassing” signs); United States v. Dunn, 480 U.S. 294, 296 (1987) (holding that officers did not violate the Fourth Amendment when they entered a 198-acre ranch without a warrant, crossing a ranch-style perimeter fence that completely encircled the property as well as several interior barbed-wire fences).

Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 5 of 17

6

“[w]hen the area searched is outside the home’s curtilage, it is irrelevant whether the officers have trespassed on the property.” United States v. Bell, 191 F. App’x 663, 667 (10th Cir. 2006). The curtilage is the area immediately surrounding the home. See Oliver, 466 U.S. at 180; Rieck, 651 F.3d at 1192. As the Supreme Court has explained, the Fourth Amendment “does not protect the merely subjective expectation of privacy, but only those ‘expectation[s] that society is prepared to recognize as ‘reasonable.’” Oliver, 466 U.S. at 177 (quoting Katz, 389 U.S. at 361). And the Supreme Court has held that there is no societal interest in protecting a person’s privacy interest in property outside the curtilage of a home, generally referred to as “open fields.” See id. at 176 (“[T]he special protection accorded by the Fourth Amendment to the people in their ‘persons, houses, papers, and effects,’ is not extended to the open fields. The distinction between the latter and the house is as old as the common law.” (quoting Hester v. United States, 265 U.S. 57, 59 (1924))). Thus, even if Plaintiff has a property interest in the land outside the curtilage of her home, an officer can enter and search that land without a warrant without violating the Fourth Amendment, even though doing so would amount to a trespass on Plaintiff’s private property. The Supreme Court has not “attempt[ed] to define the precise line between curtilage and open fields, though it did say that an open field need not be either open or a field.” Rieck, 651 F.3d at 1192 n.2 (quoting Oliver, 466 U.S. at 180 n. 11, 10).6

Plaintiff has not alleged facts showing that Alcon’s alleged trespass occurred on private property owned by her that is protected by the Fourth Amendment. In addition, however, Plaintiff also has not alleged facts to show that Alcon’s intrusion onto the property was anything other than

6 See, e.g., Rieck, 651 F.3d at 1193 (no Fourth Amendment violation where the only part of the property entered by the officer was the portion of the unpaved driveway within a few feet of the entrance gate, a considerable distance from the home which was abutting and clearly visible from a public highway); Bell, 191 F. App’x at 667 (wooded area held outside curtilage of the home, resulting in finding of no Fourth Amendment violation); United States v.

Cousins, 455 F.3d 1116, 1124 (10th Cir. 2006) (side yard held outside curtilage of the home, resulting in finding of no Fourth Amendment violation).

Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 6 of 17

7 de minimis. See United States v. Gordon, 741 F.3d 64, 72–73 (10th Cir. 2014) (“the Fourth Amendment does not proscribe all searches and seizures, but only those that are unreasonable at their inception or in their execution”; a “de minimis intrusion[ ] into a person’s possessory interest in property” may not be “constitutionally unreasonable”). Moreover, it is well established that “officers generally may ‘go to a person’s home to interview h[er],’ because ‘[i]t is not improper for a police officer to call at a particular house and seek admission for the purpose of investigating a complaint or conducting other official business.’” Wilson v. Jara, 866 F. Supp. 2d 1270, 1290 (D.N.M. 2011) (quoting United States v. Daoust, 916 F.2d 757, 758 (1st Cir. 1990), and

1 W. LaFave, SEARCH AND SEIZURE: A TREATISE ON THE FOURTH AMENDMENT § 2.3(b), at 475 (3d ed. 1996)), aff’d, 512 F. App’x 841 (10th Cir. 2013). Such encounters do not require a warrant and “need not be supported by suspicion of criminal wrongdoing.” Id. (quoting Oliver v. Woods,

209 F.3d 1179, 1186 (10th Cir. 2000)). Finally, Plaintiff has not alleged facts showing that an actual search occurred, i.e., that Alcon trespassed on Plaintiff’s property in order to gather information and not for some other reason, such as to conduct a welfare check. See Jones, 565 U.S. at 408 n.5 (stating that, “there must be conjoined with [the infringing trespass] an attempt to find something or to obtain information,” i.e., just as “the obtaining of information is not alone a search unless it is achieved by [ ] a trespass [that invades a protectible privacy interest],” an invasion of a protectible privacy interest through a physical trespass or other means “is not alone a search unless it is done to obtain information” (emphasis added)).

In short, an allegation that Alcon was trespassing on Plaintiff’s property is not sufficient to show that Plaintiff is entitled to relief under the Fourth Amendment. Rather, to proceed with her Fourth Amendment claim or claims against Alcon, Plaintiff must allege additional facts about the area of her property on which Alcon allegedly trespassed, and from which it might be inferred that Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 7 of 17

8 the area in question “harbors the intimate activity associated with the sanctity of a [Plaintiff’s] home and the privacies of life.” Oliver, 466 U.S. at 300. And she must allege additional facts about Alcon’s purpose in coming on the property or his actions when he entered the locations in question such that it is inferable that, if Alcon’s trespass did in fact infringe on a protectible privacy interest of Plaintiff’s, he did so in an attempt to obtain information and the infringement was not de minimis in nature. The current allegations only say that Alcon walked around inside Plaintiff’s yard and shined a flashlight “into the house.” The yard in question could be outside the curtilage of the house. Or Alcon could have been looking for Plaintiff not to find evidence or to obtain information about Plaintiff, but to investigate a complaint or to conduct other official business. And Alcon’s shining of the flashlight might have been no more than a de minimus intrusion on Plaintiff’s property. Without additional facts, the Court cannot say that Plaintiff has stated anything but a speculative claim for relief under the Fourth Amendment. See, e.g., SEC v. Shields, 744 F.3d 633,

640 (10th Cir. 2014) (holding that if the scope of the allegations in a complaint are so general that they encompass a wide swath of conduct, much of it innocent, then the plaintiff has not nudged her claims across the line from conceivable to plausible).

B. EQUAL PROTECTION CLAIM

The Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment provides, “No State shall ...

deny to any person within its jurisdiction the equal protection of the laws.” U.S. CONST. AMEND.

XIV, § 1. Generally speaking, “[t]he Equal Protection Clause of the Fourteenth Amendment ... is essentially a direction that all persons similarly situated should be treated alike.” City of Cleburne

v. Cleburne Living Ctr., 473 U.S. 432, 439 (1985). Thus, “[t]he Equal Protection Clause provides a basis ... for contending that general rules are being applied in an arbitrary or discriminatory way.”

Jones v. Helms, 452 U.S. 412, 423–24 (1981). “To survive a motion to dismiss, plaintiffs raising these claims must allege ‘intentional unlawful discrimination or allege facts that are at least Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 8 of 17

9 susceptible of an inference of discriminatory intent’ in their complaints.” Clark v. Washington State Dep’t of Health, 735 F. Supp. 3d 1334, 1347 (W.D. Wash. 2024) (quoting Monteiro v. Tempe Union High Sch. Dist., 158 F.3d 1022, 1026 (9th Cir. 1998)).

An equal protection claim can be established in one of two ways: a “class-based” theory or a “class-of-one” theory. A “class-based”

theory alleges that the defendant discriminated against the plaintiff on the basis of her membership in an “identifiable class,” while a “class-of-one” theory alleges that the plaintiff has been intentionally treated differently from others similarly situated and that there is no rational basis for the difference in treatment.

Id. (internal quotation marks and citations omitted).

Plaintiff alleges that Defendant Alcon violated her right to equal protection because he investigated allegations by her neighbor Todd Tatum of trespass by her onto Tatum’s property, but did nothing when she reported Todd Tatum’s trespass onto her property. Plaintiff’s allegations do not identify any class-based discrimination as a motivating factor for the alleged discriminatory treatment, and therefore she appears to be trying to allege a “class of one” claim. To adequately allege such a claim, Plaintiff must allege facts showing that she was treated differently from others similarly situated and that there is no rational basis for the difference in treatment. McGuire v.

Nielsen, 448 F. Supp. 3d 1213, 1253 (D.N.M. 2020); see Village of Willowbrook v. Olech, 528 U.S. 562, 564 (2000) (concluding that, to prevail on a “class-of-one” claim, a “plaintiff must show he or she (as opposed to a class in which he is a member) was ‘intentionally treated differently from others similarly situated’”).

In the years after the Supreme Court’s decision in Village of Willowbrook v. Olech, the Tenth Circuit expressed strong reservations about entertaining class-of-one challenges:

[T]he concept of a class-of-one equal protection claim could effectively provide a federal cause of action for review of almost every executive and administrative decision made by state actors. It is always possible for persons aggrieved by government action to allege, and

Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 9 of 17

10 almost always possible to produce evidence, that they were treated differently from others, with regard to everything from zoning to licensing to speeding to tax evaluation. It would become the task of federal courts and juries, then, to inquire into the grounds for differential treatment and to decide whether those grounds were sufficiently reasonable to satisfy equal protection review. This would constitute the federal courts as general-purpose second-guessers of the reasonableness of broad areas of state and local decisionmaking: a role that is both ill-suited to the federal courts and offensive to state and local autonomy in our federal system.

Jennings v. City of Stillwater, 383 F.3d [1199,] at 1210-11 [(10th Cir. 2004)]. See Kan. Penn Gaming, LLC v. Collins, 656 F.3d [1210,] at 1216-17 [10th Cir. 2011)] (“The latitude afforded police officers, IRS agents, university administrators, zoning officials, and other, similar government actors necessarily results in a sizeable amount of random variation in outcome. If even innocuous inconsistencies gave rise to equal protection litigation, government action would be paralyzed.”). “It is therefore imperative for the class-of-one plaintiff to provide a specific and detailed account of the nature of the preferred treatment of the favored class.” Jennings

v. City of Stillwater, 383 F.3d at 1214.

McGuire, 448 F. Supp. 3d at 1253 (emphasis added).

With these legal principles in mind, it is clear that Plaintiff’s allegations lack sufficient factual content to be able to say that she has plausibly alleged an equal protection claim. First, there is the question of standing. See Doe v. Anoka Cnty., No. 25-1568, 2026 WL 2293287, at *3 (8th Cir. Aug. 10, 2026) (holding that crime victims lack standing to assert equal protection claims against prosecutors and law enforcement officials for alleged failures to investigate (citing, inter alia, Pratt v. Helms, 73 F4th 592, 595 (8th Cir. 2023) (holding that the plaintiff lacked standing to bring § 1983 claims against officials from the Camden County Sheriff’s Department for their alleged failure to investigate an assault against him, concluding that “a victim has no constitutional right to an investigation of a crime committed against him—at least where ... the victim brings a class-of-one equal protection claim”)); see also Linda R.S. v. Richard D., 410 U.S. 614, 619 (1973) Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 10 of 17

11

(holding that a private citizen lacks a judicially cognizable interest in the prosecution or nonprosecution of another and therefore lacks standing to sue an official for his alleged failure to prosecute an alleged criminal).

Second, Plaintiff’s factual allegations suggest in only the barest of terms that she was similarly situated to Todd Tatum. See Selsor v. Workman, 644 F.3d 984, 1016 (10th Cir. 2011) (stating that “the degree to which others are viewed as similarly situated [for equal protection analysis purposes] depends substantially on the facts and context of the case, and that, consequently, many legitimate variables may serve to distinguish the plaintiff from those other persons”). In fact, even the truncated facts Plaintiff includes in the Complaint suggest a significant difference between her trespass complaint against Tatum and Tatum’s trespass complaint against her: Alcon told Plaintiff that “he didn’t know whether the road [Jaybird Lane, where Tatum’s trespass allegedly occurred] was private.” (Doc. 1-2 at 3). Plaintiff alleges Alcon’s statement showed “gross incompetence.” (Id.). But gross incompetence is not the same thing as intentional discrimination, which is what is required to state a valid equal protection claim. In any event, [t]he necessary factor making” a class-of-one equal protection claim viable is “the existence of a clear standard against which departures, even for a single plaintiff, c[a]n be readily assessed.” McGuire,

448 F. Supp. 3d at 1254 (quoting Engquist v. Or. Dep't of Agric., 553 U.S. 591, 602 (2008)). An officer’s duty to investigate reports of trespass is not subject to a clear standard against which departures can be easily assessed. See Flowers v. City of Minneapolis, Minn., 558 F.3d 794, 799-

800 (8th Cir. 2009) (pointing out that the Supreme Court in Engquist used law enforcement officers to illustrate forms of state action that by their nature involve discretionary decisionmaking). Thus, as was held in Flowers, one could conclude that a police officer’s investigative decisions simply “may not be attacked in a class-of-one equal protection claim.” Id. at 800.

Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 11 of 17

12

Third, although a police officer’s investigative decisionmay “remain subject to traditional class-based equal protection analysis,” id., such a claim would require a discriminatory or impermissible class-based motive by the officer in deciding whether, when, and how to investigate a crime. See, e.g., id. at 798 (citing St. German of Alaska E. Orthodox Catholic Church v. United States, 840 F.2d 1087, 1095 (2d Cir. 1988)). And currently Plaintiff has not alleged a sufficient claim under this framework.That is, she does not allege she is a member of a suspect class, or that a fundamental right is implicated, nor has she alleged any facts to suggest that the differing treatment she allegedly received was motivated by a class-based discriminatory purpose. See Village of Willowbrook, 528 U.S. at 564. Accordingly, as alleged, Plaintiff’s equal protection claim must be dismissed.

C. INSTRUCTIONS FOR FILING An AMENDED COMPLAINT

If Plaintiff asserts the Court should not dismiss her Fourth and Fourteenth Amendment claims against Defendant Alcon, she must file an amended complaint that complies with the Federal and Local Rules of Civil Procedure, including, as discussed above, the requirement of a “short and plain statement” of her claims “showing that [she] is entitled to relief.” In addition, Plaintiff must refrain from adding any new parties or claims to any amended complaint she files in response to this Order To Show Cause. This is because the issue of whether Plaintiff is entitled to amend the Complaint to add new parties and/or new claims cannot be addressed until after Plaintiff demonstrates that the current Complaint is not subject to dismissal. In the event Plaintiff’s Complaint is not dismissed, Plaintiff will have the opportunity to present argument that she should be allowed to add claims and/or parties to this case through a later filed motion in which she seeks leave of Court to file a second amended complaint.

Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 12 of 17

13

The Court is particularly concerned with Plaintiff attempting to add new claims and parties to this case because, as indicated by her past failed attempts to amend the Complaint, the new claims and partiesshe apparently wishes to add do not appear to have any relation to the claims in the Complaint against Defendant Alcon. Rule 18(a) provides that “a party asserting a claim … may join, as independent or alternative claims, as many claims as it has against an opposing party,” FED.R. CIV. P. 18(a), while Rule 20(a)(2) provides that “[p]ersons … may be joined in one action as defendants if: (A) any right to relief is asserted against them jointly, severally, or in the alternative with respect to or arising out of the same transaction, occurrence, or series of transactions or occurrences; and (B) any question of law or fact common to all defendants will arise in the action,” FED. R. CIV. P. 20(a)(2). Plaintiff’s previous filings lack factual allegations that plausibly connect the new claims and actions of the additional persons and entities Plaintiff seeks to sue to Plaintiff’s claims in the Complaint against Defendant Alcon as would be necessary for those new claims and parties to satisfy either Rule 18(a) or Rule 20(a)(2).

In addition, although the issue is not currently before the Court, it is questionable whether Plaintiff should be permitted to expand this lawsuit into totally new territory. Including the present lawsuit, Plaintiff has been a pro se plaintiff in eleven cases in this District over the past three years, most of which were terminated shortly after they were filed due to procedural failures on Plaintiff’s part, jurisdictional obstacles, and/or the absence of a sufficiently alleged claim for relief. Plaintiff’s lawsuits in this District include the following:

Judge Johnson’s Order Imposing Filing Restrictions states in relevant part as follows:

Plaintiff is … enjoined from initiating future litigation in this Court and the Clerk is directed to not file any initial pleading that Plaintiff submits, unless either a licensed attorney who is admitted to practice before this Court signs the pleading or Plaintiff first obtains permission to proceed pro se.

To obtain permission to proceed pro se in this Court,

Plaintiff must take the following steps:

1. File with the Clerk of Court a petition requesting leave to file a pro se initial pleading, a notarized affidavit, the proposed initial pleading, and a copy of these filing restrictions;

2. The affidavit must be notarized, be in proper legal form and recite the claims that Plaintiff seeks to present, including a short discussion of the legal bases for the claims, and the basis of the Court’s jurisdiction of the subject matter and parties. The affidavit must certify that, to the best of Plaintiff’s knowledge, Plaintiff's claims are not frivolous or made in bad faith; that they are warranted by existing law or a good faith argument for the extension, modification, or reversal of existing law; that the new suit is not initiated for any improper purpose such as delay or needless increase in the cost of litigation; and that Plaintiff will comply with all Federal Rules of Civil Procedure and the District of New Mexico’s Local Rules of Civil Procedure. If Plaintiff’s claims have previously been raised or Plaintiff has previously sued the defendants, the affidavit must certify that the proposed new suit does not present the same claims that this or any other court has decided and explain why the new suit would not be an abuse of the system;

16

3. The Clerk of the Court shall open a new civil case, file the petition, the affidavit, the proposed pleading and the copy of these restrictions in the new civil case, and randomly assign a Magistrate Judge to determine whether to grant Plaintiff’s petition to proceed pro se in the new civil case. If the Magistrate Judge approves Plaintiff’s petition to proceed pro se, the Magistrate Judge shall enter an order indicating that the matter shall proceed in accordance with the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure and the District of New Mexico’s Local Rules of Civil Procedure. If the Magistrate Judge does not approve Plaintiff’s petition to proceed pro se, the Magistrate Judge shall instruct the Clerk to assign a District Judge to the new case.

Langworthy, 1:25-cv-18-WJ-JHR, Doc. 43 at 2-3 (internal citations omitted).

In short, the purpose of this Order To Show Cause is to address deficiencies in Plaintiff’s current claims by requiring Plaintiff to file an amendment to those claims only. This Order does not grant Plaintiff leave to amend the Complaint for any other purpose, such as to add new claims or parties. Before Plaintiff may file an amended complaint that adds new claims or parties, Plaintiff must first file an amended complaint in response to this Order To Show Cause. The Court then must find that the amended complaint filed in response to this Order To Show Cause adequately pleads a claim for relief against Defendant Alcon. If the Court finds that it does, then Plaintiff may file a motion pursuant to Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 15(a)(2) seeking leave of court to file a second amended complaint, which motion must comply with the Federal and Local Rules of Civil Procedure and the Court’s previous orders. Plaintiff’s motion for leave to amend to add new claims and parties will then be addressed by the undersigned through an order setting forth proposed findings and recommended disposition on the motion, which will then be addressed by the presiding judge. For the undersigned to recommend permitting such an amended filing, Plaintiff must present legal and factual arguments in her motion seeking leave to amend for why the new claims she seeks to assert are appropriate under Rules 18 and 20, and are not barred by the restrictions imposed in Senior District Judge Johnson’s Order Imposing Filing Restrictions.

Case 1:24-cv-01291-MLG-KRS Document 64 Filed 09/18/26 Page 16 of 17

17

Again, any steps Plaintiff takes to add new claims and parties must be separate from the amended complaint that Plaintiff files in response to the present Order To Show Cause, and should not occur until after a judicial finding is made that any amended complaint Plaintiff files in response to this Order states a legally sufficient Fourth and/or Fourteenth Amendment claim against Defendant Alcon. Regardless of what the undersigned recommends, it will ultimately be up to the presiding judge to decide whether Plaintiff’s amended complaint filed in response to this Order To Show Cause adequately pleads a claim against Defendant Alcon and, if it does, whether to permit Plaintiff to file a second amended complaint that adds claims and parties beyond those included in the amended complaint. Plaintiff is forewarned that if she files an amended complaint in response to this Order that includes new claims and parties—or one that includes vexatious, abusive, or otherwise offensive language as described in the Court’s Order Striking Pro Se Filings And Revoking Permission To File Electronically, entered contemporaneously herewith—the amended complaint may be stricken from the docket and Plaintiff’s claims against Defendant Alcon dismissed without further notice.

CONCLUSION

For the foregoing reasons, Plaintiff shall, within 21 days of entry of this Order: (a) show cause why the Court should not dismiss her claims against Defendant Alcon; and (b) file an amended complaint in compliance with this Order. Failure to timely show cause and file an amended complaint may result in dismissal of this case.

IT IS SO ORDERED this 18th day of September, 2026.
